    "content": "- update-config: Use this skill to configure the Claude Code harness via settings.json. Automated behaviors (\"from now on when X\", \"each time X\", \"whenever X\", \"before/after X\") require hooks configured in settings.json - the harness executes these, not Claude, so memory/preferences cannot fulfill them. Also use for: permissions (\"allow X\", \"add permission\", \"move permission to\"), env vars (\"set X=Y\"), hook troubleshooting, or any changes to settings.json/settings.local.json files. Examples: \"allow npm commands\", \"add bq permission to global settings\", \"move permission to user settings\", \"set DEBUG=true\", \"when claude stops show X\". For simple settings like theme/model, suggest the /config command.\n- keybindings-help: Use when the user wants to customize keyboard shortcuts, rebind keys, add chord bindings, or modify ~/.claude/keybindings.json. Examples: \"rebind ctrl+s\", \"add a chord shortcut\", \"change the submit key\", \"customize keybindings\".\n- simplify: Review changed code for reuse, quality, and efficiency, then fix any issues found.\n- fewer-permission-prompts: Scan your transcripts for common read-only Bash and MCP tool calls, then add a prioritized allowlist to project .claude/settings.json to reduce permission prompts.\n- loop: Run a prompt or slash command on a recurring interval (e.g. /loop 5m /foo). Omit the interval to let the model self-pace. - When the user wants to set up a recurring task, poll for status, or run something repeatedly on an interval (e.g. \"check the deploy every 5 minutes\", \"keep running /babysit-prs\"). Do NOT invoke for one-off tasks.\n- schedule: Create, update, list, or run scheduled remote agents (routines) that execute on a cron schedule. - When the user wants to schedule a recurring remote agent, set up automated tasks, create a cron job for Claude Code, or manage their scheduled agents/routines. Also use when the user wants a one-time scheduled run (\"run this once at 3pm\", \"remind me to check X tomorrow\").\n- claude-api: Build, debug, and optimize Claude API / Anthropic SDK apps. Apps built with this skill should include prompt caching. Also handles migrating existing Claude API code between Claude model versions (4.5 → 4.6, 4.6 → 4.7, retired-model replacements).\nTRIGGER when: code imports `anthropic`/`@anthropic-ai/sdk`; user asks for the Claude API, Anthropic SDK, or Managed Agents; user adds/modifies/tunes a Claude feature (caching, thinking, compaction, tool use, batch, files, citations, memory) or model (Opus/Sonnet/Haiku) in a file; questions about prompt caching / cache hit rate in an Anthropic SDK project.\nSKIP: file imports `openai`/other-provider SDK, filename like `*-openai.py`/`*-generic.py`, provider-neutral code, general programming/ML.\n- init: Initialize a new CLAUDE.md file with codebase documentation\n- review: Review a pull request\n- security-review: Complete a security review of the pending changes on the current branch",

There are 8 threshold_onset sections (one per pair) plus a global one at line 23. Let me read the per-pair threshold_onset data to understand the structure. From there, I can compute the 3σ threshold.

But actually, the issue is that the analysis_results.json uses 2σ threshold. I need to apply 3σ. The key question is: what's the raw data needed to determine whether threshold onset is significant at 3σ?

Looking at the recompute JSON structure, for each pair there's a `threshold_3sigma_onset` with:
- canary_onset: the generation where canary metric first exceeds threshold


- downstream_onset: the generation where downstream metric first exceeds threshold  
- lead_time: downstream_onset - canary_onset
- significant: whether canary leads downstream (lead_time > 0 and both not null)

Since the 2σ thresholds in analysis_results.json won't work for 3σ, I need to recompute using the raw time series data. With 3σ, the threshold is higher, so fewer values will cross it, meaning onset detection might happen later or not at all. Let me examine one pair from the analysis_results to see the structure and figure out how to recalculate with 3σ.

The task description mentions "2/8" for Pythia-410M s43, but that's not in the recompute JSON, so I'll need to calculate it myself. I'll write a Python script to read the analysis_results.json, extract the threshold_onset data for Pythia-410M s43, and recompute the 3σ values from the raw time series.

### Assistant

_source line 89 · 2026-05-20T02:10:17.581Z_

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
No time_series.json found for pythia410m s43. Let me look for the raw data in the gen directories. The per-pair threshold_onset data should be in the analysis_results.json. Let me read the per-pair sections.

Also, I need to understand how 3σ threshold onset works. Looking at the recompute JSON, the threshold_3sigma_onset has canary_onset and downstream_onset. The "significant" means both have onset and canary leads (onset earlier).

The key insight: for 3σ, the threshold is set at mean ± 3σ instead of mean ± 2σ. 

Since onset is the first generation where the metric exceeds the threshold, a higher threshold means fewer metrics will actually trigger onset. To recompute this, I need the raw metric values per generation from the gen directories—likely in files like all_metrics.json.
```

</details>

### Assistant

_source line 96 · 2026-05-20T02:10:31.626Z_

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Now I have the raw metrics for all 11 generations of Pythia-410M s43. I need to compute the 3σ threshold onset for each canary-downstream pair.

The threshold onset method works like this:
1. For a given metric time series, compute mean and std of the first few "baseline" values (typically gen 0)
2. The onset is the first generation where the metric deviates from baseline by more than N*sigma (N=3 for 3σ)
3. For a pair (canary→downstream), threshold_onset is "significant" if:
   - Both canary and downstream have an onset
   - canary onset <= downstream onset (canary leads or simultaneous)

Looking at the scale_69b results, I see te→mauve has a canary onset at gen 2 and downstream onset at gen 6 with a 4-generation lead time, while ece→mauve shows both onsets at gen 5 and 6 respectively with just a 1-generation lead. Most other pairs never trigger a downstream onset at all. I'm using gen 0 as the baseline and computing the 3σ threshold as gen0_value ± 3*sigma for each metric.

Wait, with only a single baseline point from gen 0, I can't actually derive sigma directly from that. I need to examine how the existing analysis results structure this threshold onset calculation to figure out what method they're actually using.
```

</details>

Let me look at the existing analysis_results.json for this experiment to understand the per-pair threshold_onset data structure, and also look at how the recompute script computed it for the other 3 experiments.

From the recompute JSON, I can see the pattern:
- For each pair, `threshold_3sigma_onset` has `canary_onset`, `downstream_onset`, `lead_time`, `significant`


- `significant` is True only when both onsets are detected and the canary onset precedes the downstream onset, meaning there's a positive lead time between them.

Now I'm examining the actual onset values from the recomputed data to understand the pattern. For scale_69b, token_entropy shows a canary onset of 2 across all te→* pairs, while ece has a canary onset of 5, and the downstream metrics vary—some never crossing the 3σ threshold while mauve crosses at step 6. I need to determine the onset computation method for the Pythia-410M dataset with 11 generations to identify when each metric first exceeds 3 standard deviations.

The challenge is computing sigma from a single baseline point at generation 0. I'm thinking the approach here is either using the full time series standard deviation or treating it as relative drift from the initial noise level. Let me check the analysis code directly to see how onset is actually calculated.

Now I can see the structure. For the 2σ threshold_onset:
- te→d1: onset_canary=1, onset_downstream=2, lead_time=1, significant=true

So the onset is the first generation where the metric deviates significantly from baseline (gen 0) by more than N*sigma.

The key is understanding what "sigma" means here. Since there's only one baseline point (gen 0), the sigma must be computed differently. It's likely the standard deviation of the metric across all generations, or more likely a noise estimation from the first few points.


Looking at the actual data, token_entropy shows a dramatic drop at gen 1 under the 2σ threshold (from 3.234 to 1.793), while the 3σ threshold pushes the onset to gen 2 in the recompute data. The `_onset_gen` function probably establishes a baseline mean and standard deviation from the initial generations, then identifies the first point that crosses the threshold. Looking at the full time series for Pythia-410M s43, I can see token_entropy drops sharply from 3.234 to 1.793 in generation 1, then continues declining gradually through the remaining generations. Meanwhile, ECE climbs steadily from near zero to around 0.426, while the distinct token metrics stay relatively flat after an initial dip.

For detecting threshold onset, I'm thinking about using generation 0 as the baseline and computing sigma from the noise level in early generations or across the full series, then marking onset as the first generation where the absolute change exceeds N times sigma. Since all these metrics shift dramatically right from generation 1, I need to be careful about how I define what counts as a meaningful threshold crossing.

The tricky part is deciding what sigma actually represents—whether it's the standard deviation of the entire time series, just the stable portion after convergence, or an estimate of measurement noise. A simpler approach might be computing the mean and standard deviation across the full series and using that to define threshold boundaries, but that would trigger onset for nearly every metric. Looking at the task description mentioning changing from 2σ to 3σ in the _onset_gen function within method_ablation.py, I need to find that implementation to understand the actual approach being used.

Since I can't locate the original code, I'll reverse-engineer the onset algorithm by examining the three completed experiments I already have data for—comparing what the 3σ values are and inferring what the 2σ values would have been to understand the pattern. Looking at the standard approaches for detecting onset in time series, the most likely method uses gen 0 as a baseline and flags when a metric deviates by more than 3 standard deviations from that reference point. The challenge is figuring out how the standard deviation is calculated—whether it's measurement noise, residual variance, or something else—since we don't have multiple runs per generation to estimate it directly.

Rather than keep guessing at the methodology, I should just look for the actual analysis code that computes this threshold.

Now I have the full algorithm. The key function is `onset_gen_3sigma`:

```python
def onset_gen_3sigma(series, name):
    s = orient(series, name)  # flip sign if metric is "lower is worse"
    base, sigma = s[0], np.std(s)  # baseline is gen 0, sigma is std of entire series
    if sigma < 1e-12:
        return None
    thresh = base + 3 * sigma
    for i in range(1, len(s)):
        if s[i] > thresh:
            return i
    return None
```

The algorithm orients the series first (negating values for metrics where lower is worse like token_entropy, distinct_1/2/3, and mauve), then calculates the baseline from generation 0 and the standard deviation across the entire series. It sets a threshold at baseline plus 3 standard deviations, then scans forward to find the first generation exceeding that threshold.

Now I'm working through the actual data for Pythia-410M s43 across 11 generations, looking at token_entropy, ece, distinct_1, and distinct_2 values to apply this detection method. Compiling the metric values after applying orientation adjustments... I'm looking at the token entropy values and need to compute the standard deviation to normalize them, but rather than doing this by hand, I should just run a script on the server to recalculate everything for the Pythia-410M s43 experiment. Let me check if that experiment is already listed in the configure_experiments function.
